Re-measured with real text (corrected `sem_sim` now ~0.04-0.27, matching this channel's known scale): 6 of 50 gold days show any change at all from the entity-only baseline (44 untouched); zero breaks anywhere; +10 fixes (was +6 under the bug) vs. a content-blind "always prefer the earlier-numbered resolution" control's +9 fixes (`scripts/s6c_tiebreak_control_check.py`, unaffected by the text bug since it never reads text) across the same 50 days. Hand-verified on 4 days (`1629-10-15`, `1626-01-08`, `1627-08-19`, `1627-09-30`): the channel never unlocks a brand-new anchor in open territory -- it only resolves near-ties or small real score gaps inside gaps Group A has already narrowed (e.g. `1627-08-19`: a 4.57-vs-4.55 entity near-tie, broken by a real 0.242-vs-0.201 semantic margin, collapses a 2-into-1 forced-abstention gap into a solvable 1-into-1 one).
- **Reason:** Zero measured harm anywhere, one clearly distinctive win (`1627-08-19`) that a content-blind heuristic structurally cannot replicate, consistent in shape and magnitude with the already-shipped corpus-wide number. Not a breakthrough, but real and safe to keep. Confirms the locality principle (user, 2026-10-02, saved to memory): weak channels are separation-*resolvers*, not separation-*detectors* -- they contribute once something else has already established that a split exists nearby, not globally. Step 4b's original "relax the diagonal gate" framing is settled: already shipped, already small, already indistinguishable in aggregate from a free alternative on this sample. Re-running Phase 1b's weight calibration on the now-fixed script, to see whether 0.5 is still the right value, is a separate, not-yet-scoped follow-up.

## 2026-10-02: Group C boundary-*existence* reframing -- inconclusive, not negative; `boundary_gold_sample` is too small for this population

- **Context:** PLAN.md's queued follow-up to the Group C attribution failure above: does a phrase hit's mere *presence* inside an already-forced-abstention gap (`pigeonhole_floor > 1`) predict that gold places a real boundary there, independent of which resolution it belongs to -- a binary existence claim, not the identity-attribution claim that already failed. `scripts/s6c_group_c_boundary_existence_check.py` (new, diagnostic only) scored this directly: for every `floor > 1` gap in the live Group-A backbone across all 50 `boundary_gold_sample` days, a per-paragraph 2x2 (hit-present x gold-is-a-real-start) over paragraphs in the gap, scored only where gold locates every resolution in the gap and those locations fall inside the backbone's own window.
- **Result: the eligible population is almost empty.** 20 gaps are in scope (`floor > 1`) across all 50 days; only 2 are scoreable. Of the other 18: 9 have incomplete gold (some resolution in the gap unlocated), and **9 have gold locations that fall outside the backbone anchor's own window** -- i.e. in roughly half of these crowded gaps, the live Group-A anchor bounding the gap is itself positioned *before* where gold says the gap's own last resolution actually opens (hand-checked `1626-01-08` and `1627-08-19`: not a script bug -- `build_anchor_backbone`'s pin for the next enriched index sits at an earlier paragraph than gold's own located position for a resolution inside the gap it is supposed to bound). The 2 scoreable gaps yield only 8 paragraphs total (6 true boundaries, precision=recall=75%/100%, lift exactly 1.00x) -- not a measurement, a coin flip on n=8.
- **Decision:** Do not conclude Group C's existence-signal value either way from this sample. Do not widen the scoring window to tolerate the backbone/gold mismatch without the user's sign-off -- that changes what "inside the gap" means and risks double-counting against the neighbouring anchor's own territory, a design choice beyond what this check was asked to measure.
- **Reason:** The null result (lift 1.00x) is not evidence of "no signal" -- it is evidence of "no data yet" at this n. The secondary finding (backbone anchors themselves disagreeing with gold on exactly the crowded gaps this question targets) is new and consequential on its own: it is the same shape as the already-documented `1630-02-22` row-internal-tie/column-contention pathology (`plans/archive/STAGED_ANCHOR_CONSOLIDATION.md` "Findings in hand"), now measured at 45% (9/20) of this gold sample's `floor > 1` gaps, not just one hand-inspected day. Open question for the user: whether to (a) widen the gold sample / pathology-corpus days this check draws from to get a scoreable population, (b) redefine the paragraph window to tolerate backbone/gold disagreement (and accept the double-counting risk), or (c) close this sub-step as inconclusive and redirect effort elsewhere.

## 2026-10-02: `1626-03-28` confirmed as a secret-resolution mixup, not an annotatable boundary case -- pipeline cannot resolve this class

- **Context:** While annotating `1626-03-28` for the hard-period boundary-gold widening above, the day's `k_e=16` / `k_f=1` session (`session-4562-num-164-resolution-1`) turned out to contain, in its entire 8-paragraph / ~8,300-char HTR text, only the content of enriched entry `#15` (`162628mrt.xml#14`, Cats/Beaumont reporting to the Raad van State on the English ambassador's seat) -- the other 15 enriched entries for this date (`#1-14`, `#16`) have zero matching text in this session. User verified directly against the archival source: `https://app.goetgevonden.nl/detail/urn:republic:inv-4562-date-1626-03-28-session-164-resolution-1` and scan `NL_HANA_1_01_02_4562_0138` confirm this *is* genuinely the whole, singular, correctly-bounded secret resolution as recorded -- not a pipeline extraction error on this session's own content. The other 15 resolutions for this calendar date are real but "landed somewhere else" (a different inventory/session our per-date matching never looks at for this date).
- **Decision:** This is not an annotation task -- there is no internal boundary to mark (one resolution fills the whole visible text) and no "starts mid-resolution" fragment either (the visible resolution is complete and archivally correct). Mark `1626-03-28` in the hard-period annotation as `labelled=true`, zero markers, with a note recording the mismatch, and exclude it from any `floor > 1` boundary-existence scoring (it was not among the 5 days already flagged as good candidates for that check). Do not attempt a pipeline fix for this class of day under the current per-date `k_e`-vs-`k_f` matching design -- flagged separately, per user request, from the general severe-collapse/crowding work this session's tracks address.
- **Reason:** This is a concrete, source-verified instance of the already-parked "secret resolutions (4562/4861)" track (`PLAN.md` "Future / parked", user-parked 2026-09-29) -- not a new problem, but the first time it has been traced to an exact mechanism: the secret-series inventory holds one complete resolution per session row, while the *same calendar date's* other (non-secret) resolutions are recorded in a separate inventory/session this pipeline's date-keyed matching does not reach. Fixing it needs the cherry-picking retrieval design that track already scoped, not a boundary-annotation or `segment_day`-side change -- the pipeline's per-date `k_e`/`k_f` alignment has no mechanism to look across inventories for a single calendar date, so it will keep reporting this shape as severe collapse until that retrieval design is built.

### Correction (2026-10-02, same day): wrong mechanism for 15 of the 16 -- not secret resolutions, an unrecognized embedded session header in `resolutions_flat`

**The entry above overclaims.** Only enriched `#15` (Cats/Beaumont) is genuinely the inventory-4562 secret resolution -- that part stands, archive-verified. The other 15 "missing" enriched resolutions are not a cross-inventory secret-resolution case at all. User found enriched `1626-03-28#7` (0-indexed `#6`, Nicolaes Daems/Piet Hein appointment) verbatim online, archivally dated **27 March**, the 7th resolution of that day. Verified directly against `resolutions_flat`: that text is `session-3185-num-56-resolution-9`, dated `1626-03-27` -- inventory **3185**, a normal annual inventory, not 4562.

**Root cause found, then corrected again (same day):** `session-3185-num-56`'s own flat resolution rows span *two calendar days*. Its `resolution-8` text is, verbatim: `"President de Heer van Noortwijck, Present Sabbathi den xxviijen. Martij 1626. Oosterzee, Schaffer, Is gelesen de reqte. van Dirck Heuft ende Gerardt Thyns..."` -- a session header for **Saturday 28 March 1626**, immediately followed by content matching enriched `1626-03-28#1` (Dirck Heuft/Gerardt Thijns). First read as a `resolutions_flat`-side splitting bug (header not recognized as a day boundary). **That blame is wrong**: user confirmed `https://app.goetgevonden.nl/detail/urn:republic:inv-3185-date-1626-03-27-session-56-resolution-8` resolves correctly -- i.e. the *archive's own catalog*, not just our extraction, files this resolution under 27 March. So the embedded 28-March heading is not a boundary either `resolutions_flat` or the archive's own cataloguing recognizes; the archival "session" unit itself spans into the next calendar day's business, recorded as plain continuing text. Separately, resolution-9 bundles two logical resolutions (Daems petition + the Piet Hein appointment, enriched `#7`/`#8`) with no separator at all -- also confirmed present the same way in the archive's own resolution-9 record, not an artifact of our flat extraction. Both mismatches (session-to-day, resolution-to-paragraph) are properties of the shared archival source, not defects introduced by this pipeline's processing.

**Consequence:** 28 March 1626 genuinely has *two* real sessions -- a public one (textually present, but catalogued -- by the archive itself -- as part of 27 March's `session-56`) and a secret one (`session-4562-num-164`, correctly matched and separately dated). Our day-status resolution only ever finds the secret one for 28 March, because the public one carries the wrong date at the session level; that is why `s4_corpus_paragraph_predictions` force-fits all 15 non-secret enriched resolutions onto the single secret session, producing the degenerate `paragraph_start=paragraph_end=0` pileup for 14 of them. The real text for those 15 is not missing -- it is sitting, intact, in `session-3185-num-56`'s flat resolutions 8-17, just keyed to the wrong calendar date one level up.

**Decision:** Treat this as a `resolution-loss-census`-track finding (`PLAN.md` item 4, cross_day_shift population), not a secret-resolutions-track one, and not a "fix the splitter" bug report -- there is no local signal inside `resolutions_flat` to split on that either this pipeline or the archive's own cataloguing already recognizes. Recovering it needs either new day-boundary detection inside already-extracted session text (a new capability, not currently scoped anywhere) or accepting it as a structural gap like the secret-resolution case. Not sized corpus-wide; this is one manually-verified instance. **Open question, not yet answered:** how often an archival session unit spans two calendar days with the second day's heading landing as unrecognized plain text inside a flat resolution row -- worth a scoped check (e.g. searching `resolutions_flat.resolutions_text` for weekday-name + "den" + day-ordinal + month patterns that don't match the row's own `date` field) before concluding how common it is.
- **Reason:** Two consecutive overclaims in one investigation (first "secret resolutions", then "a `resolutions_flat` splitting bug") were each caught only because the user verified a specific resolution against the archive's own catalog rather than accepting the first plausible mechanism. The actual, narrower fact -- an archival session can itself straddle two calendar days, independent of any pipeline processing -- is a different, more fundamental constraint on this project's per-date `k_e`/`k_f` matching than either earlier framing suggested.

### Third correction, same day: a genuine extraction gap, not (only) a mis-dating -- enriched `#2-6` are absent from `resolutions_flat` entirely

User checked the session's full folio text (folio 100v) against the resolution-level breakdown and found enriched `1626-03-28#2` through `#6` (schippers op Texel, Josina/Elisabeth/Geertruijt/Diana de Flavignij, Gerart Storm, agent Brederode, the convooien/licenten pachters) have **no corresponding row anywhere in `resolutions_flat`'s `session-3185-num-56`** -- not mis-dated, not bundled, genuinely missing. Verified mapping for this session, now at verbatim-text confidence for the endpoints:

| flat row | enriched match | status |
|---|---|---|
| `resolution-8` | `#1` (Dirck Heuft) | present, bundled with the embedded 28-March session header |
| *(none)* | `#2-6` (schippers, de Flavignij, Storm, Brederode, pachters) | **absent from `resolutions_flat` entirely** -- content is on folio 100v, confirmed by user, not extracted |
| `resolution-9` | `#7` + `#8` (Daems, Piet Hein) | present, bundled with no separator |
| `resolution-10` | `#9` (Ubbinck/Abbinck) | present, clean 1:1 (verbatim match) |
| `resolution-11` to `17` | `#10-14`, `#16` | present by keyword correspondence only (not yet verbatim-checked to the same standard) |

**Settled picture for `1626-03-28` (supersedes both corrections above):** of 16 enriched resolutions, `#15` is the genuine secret resolution (separate inventory, archive-verified); `#1` and `#7-16` have real text in `session-3185-num-56` (mis-dated to 27 March, two of them bundled together); `#2-6` are a true extraction gap with no text anywhere in `resolutions_flat` -- a different, more serious failure mode than "wrong date" or "bundled without a separator." **Prevalence unknown** -- user explicitly flagged this is checked for this one session only ("I do not know about elsewhere"); do not generalize to other severe-collapse days without separately verifying each one against its own folio, the same way this one was.

**On whether `#2-6` could simply be filed elsewhere under a correct date (user, 2026-10-02):** possible but judged unlikely, by a direct argument from the parser's own demonstrated behavior rather than a new check -- the same parse that produced `resolution-8` swallowed the 28-March date announcement itself as plain inline text instead of acting on it as a boundary. A parser that fails to even recognize a date announcement when it is written out in full has no mechanism by which it could then correctly *redirect* `#2-6`'s content to a different, correctly-dated session elsewhere -- that would require exactly the date-recognition capability this instance proves is absent. The more consistent explanation is that `#2-6` were simply dropped, not mis-filed.

### Fourth instance, `1626-05-01`, then corrected to a single unifying mechanism (2026-10-05)

Same surface shape again -- `session-4562-num-181` (inventory 4562) is 1 May's one genuine secret resolution (`k_e=16`, degenerate collapse for the rest). User found the regular content is actually part of 29 April's session (`session-3185-num-82`, inventory 3185) and suspected the 1-May session start itself is missing. Confirmed via ES `session_text_regions` (page/scan data, not the unreliable `session_date` metadata field): `session-3185-num-82` spans scans 0138-0140 ending at page-278; the very next session id, `session-3185-num-83`, has **zero text regions** -- read at the time as a hollow placeholder, **which was WRONG, see the correction below**; `session-3185-num-84` then starts at page-278, the same page `num-82` ends on.

> **CORRECTION (2026-10-05, same day): `session-3185-num-83` is NOT hollow.** Our own `paragraph_axis_1626_1630` (via `axis_by_session`) holds **6 real resolutions with real text** for it (`-resolution-1` .. `-6`: the Raad van State placard on deserting soldiers, the Cleve/Cranenborch provisioning request, Gennip repair materials, an Amsterdam Admiralty missive, the Maassluis fishery request, and the Feith missive of 12 April). The "zero text regions" result came from ES `session_text_regions-2026-03-25`, which is **incomplete for that session** -- an upstream-index gap, not an absence of content. The error was trusting an upstream ES index over the corrected downstream data; `resolutions_flat` / the paragraph axis are the repaired stage, exactly as with the unreliable ES `session_date`. **Rule: never conclude "hollow session" from ES text regions alone -- check the local paragraph axis.** Consequence: 1 May's public session exists and is usable, so this date's `+1` candidate is genuinely promotable.

**User correction, same day: this is not a fourth distinct mechanism -- all four instances share one root cause.** `session_date_status_1626_1630` (`scripts/build_session_date_ledger.py`) is keyed by `(inventory_id, enriched_date)`, so every date gets one ledger row *per inventory*. Checked both `1626-05-01` and `1626-04-24` directly:

| date | inventory 3185 (public) row | inventory 4562 (secret) row |
|---|---|---|
| `1626-05-01` | `status=+1`, "one next-day HTR session candidate pending review" -> `session-3185-num-83` (the hollow placeholder above) | `status=T` (trusted) -> `session-4562-num-181` |
| `1626-04-24` | `status=?`, "multiple nearby candidates pending review" -> `session-3185-num-78`/`79` | `status=T` (trusted) -> `session-4562-num-167` |

**The ledger already correctly flags a public-inventory candidate for the date in both cases, at lower confidence.** `s4_day_status_resolution.py`'s precedence (`day_status_resolution.py`'s `resolve_row`, direct/trusted statuses win first) picks the **trusted secret match** as the single `resolved_session_id` for the whole date, so the already-identified public candidate is never reviewed or attached -- not because the pipeline fails to look for it, but because nothing downstream resolves *both* rows for a date, only one winner per date.

**Decision:** replace the "four distinct mechanisms" framing above with this single one: **a trusted secret-inventory match for a date suppresses review of that same date's already-flagged public-inventory candidate.** The varying state of the shadowed public candidate (hollow placeholder, ambiguous between two neighbors, mis-dated into an adjacent day, or a true extraction gap) is circumstantial detail *discovered after* chasing the shadowed candidate down, not a separate cause each time. For any further severe-collapse day that traces to inventory 4562/4861, check `session_date_status_1626_1630` for that date's *other*-inventory row first -- it is very likely already sitting there as a `+1`/`?`/pending candidate, and that is the fastest route to the real explanation, before re-deriving one from scratch the way the last four days each did.
- **Reason:** the first framing (four separate mechanisms, check each day fresh) would have kept costing a full investigation per day indefinitely. The real finding is mechanistic and in the pipeline's own ledger, not archival per-day trivia -- a much cheaper and more useful thing to have found.

**Sized, 2026-10-05 (user proposed the rule: "is there a single secret resolution on a day? If so, it does not count -- resolve the normal sequence first, insert it later"):** of 64 trusted (`status=T`) secret-inventory (4562/4861) ledger rows, 50 are single-resolution sessions (`k_f=1`, the population the rule targets). Checked the same-date public-inventory (3185-3189) row's status for all 50:

| public-row status | n dates | currently shadowed? |
|---|---|---|
| `E` (direct exact-date match) | 19 | **No** -- verified 5 of these (`1626-03-29`, `1626-04-10`, `1626-05-03`, `1626-05-11`, `1626-06-16`): `resolved_session_id` is already the public session, not secret. `E` already outranks `T` correctly. |
| `+1`/`-1`/`?` (pending/ambiguous) | 7 / 4 / 9 = **20** | **Yes** -- this is the exact population already demonstrated above (`1626-03-28`=`?`, `1626-04-24`=`?`, `1626-05-01`=`+1`): the secret match wins, the already-flagged public candidate is never reviewed or attached. |
| `T` (also trusted) | 6 | not checked -- a different situation (two independently-trusted candidates), not assumed to need the same fix |
| `N` (no public candidate at all) | 5 | not applicable -- nothing to shadow |

**Scope of the fix, if built:** exactly 20 dates under the narrow `k_f=1`-only framing above, not all 50 and not a corpus-wide rewrite.

### Implementation attempted and reverted, 2026-10-05: the fix cannot work as a precedence change

Delegated the status-track fix to a subagent; it landed a change in `s4_resolution_concordance.select_day_status()` (the real per-calendar-date winner selection, line 93 -- confirming the collapse happens there, not in `s4_day_status_resolution.resolve_row`, which is a genuine finding worth keeping). The change preferred *any* public-inventory row over secret. **Verified broken against real data and reverted** (`git stash`, message "haiku secret-inventory status track attempt"):

- **34 dates regressed** from `resolved_auto` (pointing at a real secret session) to `uncertain` with no session at all -- straight data loss.
- **471 further dates** changed classification, mostly `missing_htr` -> `uncertain`, degrading a specific meaningful status ("no HTR within +/-14d") to the catch-all.
- **Zero of the 20 target dates were fixed**, including all three we investigated.
- It also inverted an existing test that encoded "human approval beats automatic", so an S4f human decision on a secret inventory would lose to an automatic public match -- an unrequested override of human review.

**Why it failed -- the useful finding, and a correction to this entry's own framing above.** On the target dates the public-inventory row's `day_status` is **`uncertain`**, not a weaker candidate. The ledger's `?`/`+1`/`-1` *status codes* become `uncertain` because nothing ever **promotes** a pending-review candidate to resolved. So there is nothing for a precedence rule to prefer: demoting secret just leaves the date with no session. The design as stated ("resolve the normal sequence first") therefore needs a **promotion mechanism** -- either S4f human review of those pending rows, or a new auto-promotion rule for the unambiguous single-candidate `+1`/`-1` cases -- and precedence only matters *after* that exists.

**T3 result (2026-10-05): the 20 shadowed dates split cleanly, and promotion IS viable for over half of them.** Classified all 20 by whether their pending candidate session actually holds text, using the **local paragraph axis** (`axis_by_session`), not ES:

| class | n | dates |
|---|---|---|
| exactly one candidate **with text** -- unambiguous, auto-promotable | **11** | `1626-05-01`, `1626-09-14`, `1626-12-16`, `1626-12-19`, `1627-01-19`, `1627-04-06`, `1627-05-26`, `1627-09-10`, `1627-10-21`, `1627-10-27`, `1628-12-15` |
| two candidates with text -- genuinely ambiguous, needs the review that never happened | **9** | `1626-03-28`, `1626-04-24`, `1626-05-25`, `1627-01-05`, `1627-08-27`, `1627-09-12`, `1627-10-06`, `1628-04-24`, `1629-01-22` |
| all candidates hollow / no text | **0** | -- |

**Supersedes the earlier claim that "promoting either yields no usable text"** -- that rested on the hollow-`num-83` error corrected above. Every one of the 20 dates has at least one candidate carrying real text. So: an auto-promotion rule for the single-candidate case would address **11 dates** with no human review at all; the remaining **9** need an adjudication between two text-bearing neighbours (`1626-04-24`: `num-78` 23 paragraphs vs `num-79` 18; `1626-03-28`: `num-56` 21 vs `num-57` 2). Candidate paragraph counts are recorded per date in the T3 run and are the natural input to ranking that review.

**Design sharpened further (user, same day): this is the real justification for recovering the per-resolution `secret` flag (`project_secret_resolutions` memory), not just faster triage.** Rather than special-casing `k_f=1` trusted secret matches, give secret-inventory (4562/4861) matches their **own status track** in `s4_day_status_resolution.py`, outside the `T`/`E`/`?`/`+1`/`-1` ladder public-inventory candidates compete in. A secret match then never becomes a date's `resolved_session_id` by default regardless of its own `k_f` -- the normal-inventory sequence always resolves first, and the secret resolution is attached separately afterward. This also cleanly covers the 6 `T`/`T` dates (both inventories independently trusted) that the narrow `k_f=1` rule left unchecked/ambiguous, since under this design secret status never enters that comparison at all. Not yet implemented -- awaiting a decision on whether to scope this as a code change now or record and defer.

**Confirmed directly (user's framing: "if it is not in the session's text it cannot have been parsed"):** pulled `session-3185-num-56`'s raw 21-paragraph HTR axis via `axis_by_session` (`scripts/build_pathology_corpus.load_axis_indexes`) -- the per-paragraph representation independent of `resolutions_flat`'s own resolution-level splitting, i.e. a layer closer to the original ingestion. Searched for distinctive terms from both sides: `daems`, `pieterssen hein`, `heuft`, `boxhoorn`, `abbinck` (the confirmed-present resolutions) all appear; `texel`, `flavignij`, `josina`, `storm`, `brederode`, `pachters`, `konvooien` (the `#2-6` content) appear **nowhere** in the raw axis. So the gap is not a `resolutions_flat`-side resolution-splitting defect -- the content is absent even one layer below that, in the project's own raw per-paragraph HTR text. This closes the question for this instance: it is an upstream ingestion gap (OCR/HTR/page-range), not something any downstream parsing or splitting fix in this pipeline could recover.

## 2026-10-05: Recover secret flags from source XML

- **Context:** `enriched_resolutions_1626_1630` has a `secret` boolean field that is `False` on all 19,121 rows. The value was dropped during derivation from the source XML archive (`data.bak/resoluties_staten_generaal_1626-1630.zip`), which contains 1,594 day files with metadata marking resolutions and resolution-parts as secret.
- **Decision:** Extract per-resolution `secreet_resolutie` and `secreet_deel_count` flags from the source XML, plus register citations from `<noot>` elements, and publish as a new join-ready dataset `enriched_resolution_secret_flags` (JSONL, semi-structured phase). Published only the 381 resolutions with at least one marker (vs. all 19,120) for economy and scanning efficiency.
- **Results:** Extraction totals exactly match given figures: 106 `secreet_resolutie`, 18 `secreet_deel` markers across 124 total markers in 117 day files (all 1,594 day files processed, 1,594 resolutions per day verified to 19,120 total). Known-case validation passed: `1626-03-28` resolution_index 14 (Cats/Beaumont English ambassador's seat): correctly flagged with `secreet_deel_count=1` and `register_citations=['3906']`. ~~One date mismatch noted: `1629-11-15` (26 enriched resolutions but 13 in XML), recorded and logged.~~ **This claim is wrong, corrected on verification 2026-10-05:** `enriched_resolutions_1626_1630` holds **13** rows for `1629-11-15` (file `162915nov.xml`), matching the XML's 13, and **zero** rows corpus-wide come from `.bak` files -- i.e. `scripts/regenerate_enriched_resolutions.py`'s `.xml.bak` dedup has in fact been applied. There is no mismatch on this date. (Separately verified: the 4 day files whose `<zittingsdag>` header uses a different attribute order or has trailing whitespace in a value -- `162724au.xml`, `162622mei.xml`, `162616mei.xml`, `162626juli.xml` -- were handled correctly by this extraction; `1626-05-22`'s `secreet_resolutie` marker is present in the output. An initial verification pass that reported them as dropped was using a stricter date regex than the extraction itself.) Dataset registered in `data_manifest.toml` as parent `enriched_resolutions_1626_1630`; `data_io.check` passes.
- **Reason:** Enables future secret-inventory tracking refactor in `s4_day_status_resolution.py` (design in PLAN.md 2026-10-05 and 2026-10-02 decisions) without requiring the slow re-extraction pathway. Positioning secret match resolution outside the main public-inventory ledger depends on knowing which resolutions are actually secret at the source level, not just guessing by registry inventory. Zero-cost extraction: positional join on day files to enriched data is 1:1 by design, no ambiguity or alignment risk.

## 2026-10-05: T2 built -- per-resolution archival provenance from ES, no text join needed

- **Context:** User asked for provenance on each resolution (which manuscript page/scan carries it), secret ledger first, as a mechanical task separate from the secret-flag recovery. A delegated pre-check reported the job infeasible, claiming ES stores no resolution text, that our ids don't resolve, and that `resolutions_flat` and the ES indices come from "incompatible sources". **All three claims were wrong** and were discarded after direct verification: reading the `full_resolutions-2026-03-25` mapping shows the text sits at `paragraphs[].text` (the agent queried a non-existent top-level `text` field), and -- decisively -- `metadata.page_ids` is carried **on each resolution document**, so provenance needs no text matching at all.
- **Decision:** Built `scripts/build_resolution_archival_provenance.py` -> dataset `resolution_archival_provenance` (registered, `data_io.check` passes). One row per flat resolution id in inventories 3185-3189 + 4562, carrying `inventory_num`, `page_num`, `page_ids`, and `scan_ids` derived by stripping the `-page-NNN` suffix. Two deliberate omissions, both documented in the manifest entry: ES `session_metadata.session_date` is not carried (uncorrected first-pass output; `resolutions_flat`'s date is the corrected value), and resolution text is not pulled in bulk. Unresolved ids are written with `found = false` rather than dropped, so the coverage gap is visible in the data.
- **Results:** 13,052 in scope, **9,356 resolved (71.7%)**; of those, **100% carry `page_ids`**. By inventory: 3187 91.3%, 4562 **87.8%**, 3189 76.3%, 3188 67.8%, 3185 62.6%, 3186 59.8%. Note 4861 is nominally a secret inventory but has **zero rows in `resolutions_flat`**, so it contributes nothing. Integrity checks both pass at 100% of resolved rows (9,356/9,356): ES `inventory_num` agrees with the inventory encoded in our own flat id, and every `scan_id` sits in that same inventory. **Independent validation:** `session-4562-num-164-resolution-1` (the Cats/Beaumont secret resolution) returns scans `4562_0138`/`0139`/`0140` -- the user had independently cited scan `NL_HANA_1_01_02_4562_0138` for that exact resolution from the archive web app, before this dataset existed.
- **Reason:** This is the first channel in the project that can point a specific resolution at a specific manuscript image, which is what makes a contested placement or date adjudicable by a human rather than only arguable. It also supplies the honest provenance basis the earlier "is there a structural difference with the secret ledgers" question needs -- that question was previously answered only via matched-session `inventory_id`, which is an artifact of session matching rather than real provenance. **Left deliberately (user, 2026-10-05):** the 3,696 ids (28.3%) that do not resolve in this ES snapshot, most likely because our flat resolution numbering is finer than ES's in places. Accepted rather than chased, consistent with the upstream-fault stopping rule -- the gap is visible in the data as `found = false`, so it can be revisited if it ever blocks something. Do not re-open it speculatively.

## 2026-10-05: Group C boundary-existence check widened to the hard-period gold -- the metric is structurally degenerate in `floor > 1` gaps, not merely under-powered

- **Context:** Follow-up to 2026-10-02's inconclusive existence check, taking option (a) from that entry: widen the gold population. The user's hard-period annotation progressed (5 of the 5 prioritized `floor > 1` candidate days now carry located cuts) and was merged into `boundary_gold_hard_period_sample`, so `scripts/s6c_group_c_boundary_existence_check.py` was extended to read both samples and report each separately plus combined. The script is the first consumer anywhere of three conventions the hard-period annotation introduced: `date_start`/`date_end` slots (the HTR session spans two calendar dates; paragraphs outside the bracket are clipped out of the scored window, which *narrows* it and is therefore not the window-widening 2026-10-02 deferred), `no_htr_text` cuts (already unlocated by `is_located`, so they make their gap incomplete rather than inventing a start), and days annotated as having nothing locatable, which the merge leaves indistinguishable from unannotated days because it stamps `annotated_at` on every day it touches. `1626-03-28` is excluded by name per the 2026-10-02 decision.
- **Result: the original sample reproduces exactly, the widening works as a widening, and the measurement still cannot discriminate.** The 50-day block reproduces 2026-10-02 to the digit (20 gaps in scope, 2 scoreable, 8 paragraphs, 6 real boundaries, precision 75% / recall 100%, lift 1.00x) -- a usable regression check on the extension. The hard-period sample is exactly the dense population it was built to be: **21 in-scope `floor > 1` gaps from 24 days, versus 20 from 50 original days** (0.88/day vs 0.40/day). But only 2 of the 21 are scoreable: 11 sit on the 14 days annotated with nothing locatable, 8 more on partially-labelled days whose cuts are recorded as `no_htr_text`, and 5 of the 24 days have no HTR/enriched data at all. Those 2 gaps yield 4 paragraphs, of which **4 of 4 are real boundaries -- a base rate of exactly 100%** (precision 100%, recall 50%, lift 1.00x). Combined: 41 in scope, 4 scoreable, 12 paragraphs, base rate 83.3%, precision 80% / recall 80%, lift 0.96x. No paragraph was clipped by a `date_start`/`date_end` bracket on current data.
- **Decision:** Stop treating sample size as this check's blocker, and do not annotate further hard-period days *for this question*. The `floor > 1` window admits almost no negatives by construction, so the existence 2x2 is close to unmeasurable there regardless of n.
- **Reason:** `floor > 1` is *defined* as `gap_count > room` -- more unplaced resolutions than paragraphs to hold them -- so pigeonhole forces nearly every paragraph in the window to open a resolution. There is almost no continuation text in the window to be wrong about, the base rate runs to 100% (and did, exactly, on the hard sample), and since lift is `precision / base_rate` its ceiling is `1/base_rate` ~ 1.0. A detector that answered "boundary" at every paragraph would score precision 100% in this population. Finishing the remaining 14 annotatable hard-period days would add roughly 11 scoreable gaps and a few dozen paragraphs at the same pinned ceiling: more n, no more discrimination. This supersedes option (a) of the 2026-10-02 entry ("widen the gold sample") as the way out; the obstacle is the window definition, not the data volume. Option (b) (redefine the window) remains unauthorized and is now also unattractive, since widening the window to admit negatives would import the neighbouring anchor's territory -- the double-counting risk already flagged. The live candidate successor is therefore the `floor == 1` population, where `room >= gap_count` leaves spare paragraphs, the base rate sits well below 100%, and lift is measurable again; it is also where the locality principle predicts a weak channel pays, as a resolver of a near-tie inside an already-narrowed gap rather than a detector of a split. Caveat to carry into that step: `segment_gap` does not abstain in `floor == 1` gaps, so an existence signal there must *beat* today's placement rather than fill a void, which makes it a tie-break comparison. Note that `s6c_group_c_attribution_check` already scored `floor == 1` gaps but asked the *identity* question (30.9% vs 47.9% baseline); the existence question has not been asked in that population.
- **Side note on the 2026-10-02 backbone/gold finding:** the hard sample shows 0 "gold outside the backbone window" skips against the original sample's 9 of 20, but this is *not* evidence the crowding pathology is absent there -- its 8 partially-labelled gaps are skipped as incomplete before the window test is ever reached. The 45% figure stands as measured on the original sample only.

## 2026-10-05: `separated` rules over gold correctness when they disagree (user ruling) -- and the three blocked changes are now measured, with very different costs

- **Context:** Open since 2026-09-29/30 and logged in `PLAN.md` track 2 as blocking any placement change: the gated TF-IDF wiring gains `separated` corpus-wide (+315) but *loses* gold correctness against the ungated variant (exact 0.5865 ungated vs 0.5321 gated), so the two criteria disagree in sign. By 2026-10-05 the same question gated three separate changes: this track's gate choice, the secret-session hijack withdrawal (224 fabricated placements), and duplicate-session dedup (1,884 resolutions).
- **Decision (user, 2026-10-05): `separated` rules. The gold is too limited.** Where the `separated` criterion (`label_separation`: unique start paragraph on the date, extent <= 3) and gold start-paragraph correctness disagree, `separated` adjudicates. Gold remains valuable as a *diagnostic* -- it is how mechanisms get understood and how instruments get calibrated -- but it does not decide corpus-wide placement questions.
- **Reason:** `separated` is measurable on all 13,374 placed rows; gold exists for 50 + 25 days, of which few are usable for any given question. This session demonstrated the limit concretely rather than in the abstract: the widened Group C existence check, run across all 74 gold + hard-period days, could score **12 paragraphs in total** (4 gaps), and the hard-period half returned a base rate of exactly 100%, where no discrimination is possible at any sample size. A criterion that cannot be evaluated on the population being changed cannot adjudicate changes to it.
- **Measured consequences, same day (the three changes are NOT equally affected):**
  - **Track 2 gate:** keep the gated wiring (+315 `separated`), accepting the correctness loss on gold. Applies directly.
  - **Secret-session hijack -- effectively free, now UNBLOCKED.** Of inventory 4562's 318 placed rows, only **6 are `separated`**; 307 fail on `collision`, 3 on `collision_and_extent`, 2 on `extent`. So withdrawing the fabricated paragraph-0 pileup costs at most **6 of the corpus's 8,084 separated rows (0.07%)**. Of those 6, **2 are the flagged secret resolutions the mechanism re-attaches anyway** (`1627-07-11` #0/#1, the one day carrying two flags); the other 4 (`1627-10-06` #2/#3/#4, `1628-12-15` #13) are unflagged and need a per-row decision rather than blanket withdrawal. This is the criterion behaving *correctly*: 307 collided placements already score zero, so `separated` declines to reward the fabrication on its own.
  - **Duplicate-session dedup -- real exposure, still constrained.** Of the 820 placed rows on duplicate-claimed sessions, **428 are `separated`** -- 5.3% of the corpus total. Dedup is therefore not a free change under this ruling, and its design gains a constraint: where tiers 1-2 (monotonicity brackets, secret flag) do not decide, **prefer the claimant whose rows are `separated`**. The true loss is smaller than 428, since the winning claimant keeps its placements; 428 is the exposure, not the bill.
- **One guard this ruling needs, and why it does not weaken it:** `separated` is gameable by a degenerate placement -- Step 2 found a trivial uniform spread beats gold's own annotations on this criterion. So `separated` counts must continue to be reported against the uniform null (`uniform_start_paragraphs`), never in absolute terms. The ruling settles which metric *adjudicates* a disagreement; it does not make an unbeaten-by-uniform gain into a win. Note that nothing about the three changes above trips this: the hijack withdrawal *removes* degenerate placements, and dedup removes duplicate claims rather than spreading placements.

## 2026-10-05: Duplicate sessions are overwhelmingly *shared*, not contested -- heading channel decides 37%, after two failed designs

- **Context:** 67 of 1,053 resolved sessions are claimed by more than one enriched date (1,884 enriched resolutions, 1,064 of the corpus's 1,137 `cross_day_shift` rows, claims always within 5 days). User framing: disambiguate **as anchors, not as ownership** -- an archival session can legitimately carry two calendar days, as `session-3185-num-56` does for 27/28 March 1626 with the 28th's heading inside it as plain text. So the operation is to locate each claimant's region inside the session, not to award the session. The user identified the embedded heading as the signal; `scripts/s6_duplicate_session_date_headings.py` (new, diagnostic only) runs that channel.
- **Result: 25 of 67 (37%) decided, and every decided case is `shared` -- zero spurious.** 5 `shared_confirmed` (both claimants with their own verified heading), 20 `shared_one_heading` (the later claimant's heading sits below the session opening; the earlier claimant's heading *is* the session header). Remaining 42: 23 `no_heading_signal`, 18 `hits_but_none_verified`, 1 `one_claimant_heading_only`. The anchors framing is vindicated empirically -- an ownership model would have discarded real content on all 25.
- **Two failed designs, both caught by a held-out case rather than by inspection.** The validation case is the user's archive-verified finding that `session-3185-num-56` contains a 28 March heading, and it is genuinely held out: our ledger gives that session a *single* date, so it is not among the 67.
  - **v1 (fuzzy-match each candidate date's full phrase): reported 43/67 (64%), an artifact.** Adjacent days' Roman numerals (`xxviij`/`xxix`/`xxxj`) differ by one character and sit inside any useful fuzzy threshold, so one heading matched several candidate dates at the same offset. Checked directly: **0 of 41 apparent "both headings present" verdicts had the claimants' hits at separate sites** -- 28 were one heading matched twice, 13 partially contaminated. `session-3185-num-22` exposed it: paragraph 0 reads `'J. ovis den xxixen. Januarij 1626'` (the 29th) and matched both the 28th (0.90) and the 31st (0.94), neither being its date.
  - **v2 (one wildcard frame `"den .. Martij 1626"`, parse the day off the match): worse.** With a frame that generic the *year* matches fuzzily too -- `'ende vjen Decembris 1623'` matched the 1626-1630 frames simultaneously -- and the validation heading was missed entirely. 1/67, unproven.
  - **v3, shipped: fuzzy match to LOCATE, exact lookup to VERIFY, weekday as an independent cross-check.** Stage 1 keeps HTR tolerance (validation heading found at 0.98); stage 2 looks the day token up exactly; stage 3 reads the weekday preceding the match. **376 fuzzy hits are rejected by stage 2** -- that number *is* v1's false-positive mass, now measured.
- **Vocabularies come from the REPUBLIC project, not hand-rolled (user: "er is hiervoor code in het republic project").** `scripts/_republic_date_vocab.py` is a generated copy of `republic/model/republic_date_phrase_model.py` at commit `3b46b43b`, carrying the *handwritten* weekday and month variants and the full day table. This matters concretely: the handwritten set has forms a hand-rolled vocabulary would miss (`Mercurij`, `Mercury`, `Sabati`, `Saterdach`, `Doica`, `Sondach`), and the day table decodes HTR confusions by exact lookup (`1xen` -> 9, `vjen` -> 6) instead of Roman arithmetic -- `vjen` is what `'ende vjen Decembris 1623'` actually says, so that string is 6 December and never was a 1626-1630 date.
  - **Copied, not imported, and `uv add` is not available here:** `republic-project` pins `python = "^3.8,<3.12"` (this project runs 3.12.13) and `fuzzy-search = "^2.6.0"` while this project depends on fuzzy_search 3.0.3, so installing it would downgrade the searcher `build_combined_c_searcher` and the Group C phrase work rely on. Those two pins are the blockers if the dependency is ever wanted properly.
- **Weekday cross-check (user suggestion) measured, not assumed:** readable on **354 of 422** fuzzy hits (84%), it **vetoed 3** hits whose day matched but whose weekday disagreed and **rescued 11** whose day token would not parse but whose weekday agreed -- 14 corrections the numeral alone got wrong or lost, moving the session count 22 -> 25. Calendar alignment was checked before relying on it: `1626-03-28` is written `Sabbathi` and `pd.Period` gives Saturday, `1626-01-29` is written `Jovis` and gives Thursday, so no Julian correction is needed (n=2, not a proof).
- **Decision:** Keep the three-stage design, and keep the held-out validation as a precondition on any future heading work -- a heading channel that cannot recover `session-3185-num-56` is not to be trusted however good its aggregate numbers look. Treat the 42 undecided as the entity channel's population, not as evidence of anything. No placement or status change yet.
- **Reason:** This is the third instance in one day of a single failure mode -- a channel that *detects* something real, forced into a *hard* decision it cannot support (Group C attribution 30.9% vs 47.9% baseline; the existence 2x2 in windows with no negatives; heading attribution's 376 collisions). Detection and attribution are different problems, and conflating them turned 0% into an apparent 64%. Two independent weak constraints (exact day, weekday) composed cleanly where one fuzzy score did not, which is the concrete argument for calibrating the scoring the pipeline already performs rather than adding further hard rules -- see the probabilistic-anchors note in `PLAN.md`.

## 2026-10-05: P1 PASS: Group C interior offsets beat the uniform null, but only with single-word phrases dropped

- **Context:** Track P step P1 (PLAN.md main line 2026-10-05): free pre-check on whether Group C can place a cut INSIDE a paragraph, not merely offer a candidate there. Scored on the 81 gold mid_paragraph opening slots across 33 days in boundary_gold_sample + boundary_gold_hard_period_sample (8 at char_offset 0 held out as degenerate -> 73 interior cuts, 63 host paragraphs), against exact closed-form count-matched uniform-random-position nulls inside the same paragraph. Channel calibration, not a track metric; displacement reported continuously, no tolerance-threshold F1.
- **Decision:** PASS on the >=3-word channel, so P2 (lumped_paragraph_gold_scaffold annotation) is licensed. Restrict Group C to phrases of >=3 words: build_combined_c_searcher currently takes s2_anchor_phrase_inventory's top 20 unfiltered, so single-word phrases are live members and the channel as defined fails its own usable facet. Do NOT wire Group C offsets as a standalone cut placer.
- **Reason:** Nearest-candidate displacement 120.4 chars vs count-matched null 246.7 (ratio 0.49), 47/57 cuts beat their own null (sign test p<0.0001), median 40 chars, p25 = 1 char, exact on 13/57; monotone one-to-one assignment agrees at 0.45 (39/47, p<0.0001). But the channel's own highest-similarity pick -- what production would do without gold -- lands 351.7 vs 448.7 (ratio 0.78, 38/57, p=0.0082), median 209 chars off, exact on 3/57, and selects its closest-to-gold candidate only 33/57 times: the set carries the cut, the ranking cannot find it. Unfiltered, the ranked facet is 1.01 (39/69, p=0.168), indistinguishable from a random position, so the >=3-word restriction is what makes the channel pass, at a cost of availability 94.5% -> 78.1% and median 4 -> 2 candidates per cut. Locality principle, now measured on character offsets: Group C is a cut resolver inside an already-narrowed window, never a cut selector.

## 2026-10-05: SUPERSEDES today's P1 calibration call: Group C's defect is vocabulary and typing, not scoring or phrase length

- **Context:** User-led diagnosis following the P1 run (same day). The P1 measurement stands; its proposed fix does not. Group C's inventory was built by n-gram frequency OVER known openings (s2 top-20 n-grams, s4's 271 count-ranked candidates) with no background frequency ever consulted, so it conflates real formulas, truncated prefixes of formulas, and free prefixes shared with ordinary prose. Frequency cannot separate them: 'in deliberatie geleyt synde,' (genuine, gold-confirmed) has count 5 while 'de heeren van' (never an opening formula) has count 10.
- **Decision:** Withdraw the >=3-word phrase filter recommended earlier today -- 'de heeren van' is three words and passes it. Do not pursue a scoring or specificity threshold either: opening-rate against the 12,811 para_index==0 openings scores the correct formula at 0.059, because that formula mostly opens resolutions mid-paragraph and the para_index==0 corpus comes from the same under-segmented stream Track P exists to fix. Instead rebuild the Group C generator as a typed, period-filtered formula searcher: REPUBLIC's resolution_phrase_model (republicgit @ 25924b93) for structural typology and class, our empirical n-grams as variants attached to sanctioned frames, the participial construction as a pattern rather than a phrase list, max_start_offset honoured, intake-before-decision ordering applied. Then re-run P1 unchanged on the same 73 cuts. Needs user sign-off before code.
- **Reason:** Three measurements, each decisive. (1) The inventory is fit to the wrong population: s4_opening_phrase_cut_evaluation_full shows the fixed-phrase inventory covers 85.4% of paragraph_boundary gold cuts but only 61.2% of mid_paragraph ones, and the uncovered mid-paragraph openings are overwhelmingly one productive participial construction (Synde/Syn + participle, optionally + ter Vergaderinge) -- frame head covers 35.8% of mid_paragraph vs 16.6% of paragraph_boundary cuts. That is why the bare stem 'synde' (count 740) is in the inventory: frequency captured the stem of a productive frame instead of the frame. (2) Checked against the 13,374 baseline-identified openings, the formulas that matter under-score exactly as the user predicted: participial 12.5%->28.4% (ratio 2.27), inverted <participle> synde 4.7%->13.4% (2.88), ter vergaderinge gecompareert 2.4%->6.0% (2.46), against ontfangen 17.2%->3.0% (0.17) and is/synde gelesen 12.2%->4.5% (0.37). The three under-detected frames are 48% of gold mid-paragraph openings but 19.6% of baseline. Baseline coverage therefore measures what is already visible and is never evidence of a formula's weight. (3) User's two structural rules confirmed corpus-wide: intake precedes decision 97.0% (3,529/3,637), and a decision formula opens only when no intake is available 96.7% (1,097/1,134). Under typing plus ordering the worked example resolves with no tuning: 1629-09-22 para 4 picks offset 1640 ('in deliberatie geleyt synde,', REPUBLIC-sanctioned as a 1576-1630 frame) at 1 char from gold, where similarity ranking picked 'de heeren van' (sim 1.000) and was 1,249 chars wrong. Remaining gap: 40.3% of gold mid-paragraph openings are unclassified by the 7 frames, so the early-period inventory is described, not complete.

## 2026-10-05: Typed Group C rebuilt and re-measured on P1's fixed population -- `ranked` moves from marginal to established; P3 licensed

- **Context:** The rebuild signed off in the preceding entry, implemented as `scripts/s6c_group_c_typed_formula_channel.py` with REPUBLIC's vocabulary copied to `scripts/_republic_resolution_phrase_vocab.py` (commit `3b46b43b`, byte-identical at the cited `25924b93`; `uv add` blocked by REPUBLIC's `python <3.12` and `fuzzy-search ^2.6.0` pins, same blockers as `_republic_date_vocab.py`). `s6c_group_c_interior_offset_calibration.py` now takes `--channels`; population, coordinate check, nulls, facets and day-clustering are untouched, so only the channel moves. The legacy arms are the regression check and reproduce the recorded figures exactly: `legacy` ranked ratio 1.01, `legacy:3` nearest 0.49 / ranked 0.78 / assignment 0.45, selector 33/57, availability 94.5% -> 78.1%, 81 slots -> 73 interior cuts over 33 days, coordinate check 30/30.
- **Decision:** Adopt the typed channel as Group C's candidate generator for Track P. The `ranked` facet -- the only one that decides anything, because production has no gold to pick with -- goes from day-clustered 18/27 days p=0.061 (CI [0.623, 0.958], direction only) to **26/32 days p=0.0003, ratio 0.62, CI [0.403, 0.822]**, with availability *rising* 78.1% -> 95.9% rather than being traded away. That is the stated goal of the rebuild met, so P3 (carry the offset through `build_anchor_backbone`/`segment_gap`) is licensed on accuracy as well as availability. Still not a standalone cut placer: `nearest` (0.40) remains well below `ranked` (0.62), so the locality principle holds -- resolver inside a narrowed window, not detector.
- **Reason:** Measured on the same 73 cuts. `nearest` 91.1 chars vs count-matched null 226.9 (ratio 0.40, 29/32 days, p<0.0001, CI [0.219, 0.546]), median **1 char**, exact on 21/70 against legacy:3's 13/57. `ranked` 261.4 vs 424.2, median 71 chars against 209, exact on 16/70 against 3/57. `assignment` 0.32 (29/31 days, p<0.0001). Selector agreement 33/57 -> 46/70. Ablations attribute the gain and one of them is negative: the typed candidate *set* under the **old** similarity ranking already reaches ranked 0.70 (27/32, p=0.0001, CI [0.549, 0.826]), and the typed ranking adds 0.70 -> 0.62 with selector 34/70 -> 46/70 -- so both halves of the diagnosis were real, vocabulary and selection. The participial pattern buys availability and precision, not ratio: dropping it costs 95.9% -> 83.6% availability and nearest median 1 -> 16 chars at an unchanged ranked 0.63. **The intake-before-decision ordering rule contributes nothing measurable here** (ranked 0.62 and CI [0.406, 0.822] with it off, identical); it suppresses 65 of 71 decision-class candidates and it is what makes the worked example resolve, but on this population its effect on displacement is nil, and it should be described as typing hygiene rather than as a measured accuracy mechanism. Two bugs found and fixed while validating against the worked example, both now regression-documented in the module: an offset's class must be settled by whichever match realises *most* of a sanctioned formula (class-blind) **before** the ordering rule runs, or REPUBLIC's 2-token `Is goetgevonden` types the 4-token decision formula at `1629-09-22`:1669 as an intake and shields it from suppression; and a participial match whose span crosses a clause terminator is not one construction (`gedelibereert synde, is goetgevonden ende verstaen` was matched forward as `synde ... goetgevonden` and typed intake). The worked example now resolves as predicted: 16 raw matches -> 7 offsets -> 3 interior candidates, `de heeren van` purged at admission as `prefix_phrases: de Heeren` (REPUBLIC's own `person_name_prefix`, categorical rather than thresholded), 1200/1669 typed decision and suppressed, pick = offset 1640, **1 char from gold**. Caveat on the figures: run at `--reps 300`. The `nearest` and `ranked` facets are unaffected because their nulls are closed-form, but the `assignment` null and every bootstrap CI are Monte Carlo at 300 draws; the authoritative run is the default `--reps 10000`. Free parameters, none fitted to gold: `TOKEN_SIMILARITY` 0.72 (the searcher's own 0.85 is too strict per token), `MAX_CANDIDATE_SKIP` 2 (`syn in de vergaderinge gecompareert` writes `in de` where the frame writes `ter`), `MAX_LEAD_SKIP` 1 (`noch ontfangen een missive van` realises the frame in full behind one adverb).

## 2026-10-06: P2 closed at 12/80 -- proven-pairs gold preferred over more hand labeling; Group A pivot withdrawn on inspection

- **Context:** Follow-up to the P2 tooling fix (same session, prior entry absent because this is the first write today). The user's reaction to the 13-row prefix's thin yield projection (~13 cut-bearing of 80, CP95 [2, 39]) was to doubt whether more hand annotation earns its cost, and to prefer "a gold set from proven pairs" instead.
- **First proposal, withdrawn before building anything.** `s6b_anchor_harvest`'s `tier1_entity_nw` (Group A) carries 1,098 `(date, inventory, char_position)` positions where 2+ distinct `enriched_index` values collide -- read at first as 1,689 proven interior cut positions, free of annotation. Wrong on inspection of `scripts/s6b_known_point_ledger.py`: `tier1_points` sets `char_position = flat_id_char_starts[row.flat_id]`, the start offset of the **flat resolution's paragraph**, not a per-mention offset. Two enriched indices sharing one `char_position` means two enriched resolutions matched the *same flat paragraph* -- i.e. this is the already-known collision count (P1's own docstring reports 1,597 colliding paragraphs) restated, with every colliding row pinned to the identical offset. No second position exists in this data; it carries zero information about where inside the paragraph a true split falls.
- **Decision: close P2 at 12 gold / 80 (not continued to 80), and point future offset-accuracy work at `synthetic_boundary_*` as the proven-pairs gold set, which already exists.** `docs/steps/STEP_SYNTHETIC_BOUNDARY_ENCODER.md`'s pipeline concatenates pairs of consecutive, independently-`separated`-confirmed resolutions (concordance's own `label_separation()`, not a human call) with no separator; the true boundary is the exact character length of the first segment, known by construction. `synthetic_boundary_folds` already holds 2,041 rows at zero further labor, vs lumped's 80 (~13 cut-bearing).
- **Reason, and the real cost of this route (already measured, not new):** `docs/SYNTHETIC_BOUNDARY_GOLD50_TRANSFER.md` found transfer from this synthetic distribution to genuinely lumped paragraphs tops out at F1@50 = 0.259 (`GloBERTise`) -- concatenation-of-separates is an easier, out-of-distribution task relative to a real lumped HTR paragraph (no shared seam vocabulary, no soft transition). So the proven-pairs set is plentiful and free but not a substitute for the in-distribution signal; the 12 lumped rows (2 `cuts`, 10 `no_cut`) remain the only in-distribution check and are kept as a diagnostic, just not worth extending by hand to 80.
- **Not yet done:** redirect P3's offset-carrying test (`build_anchor_backbone`/`segment_gap`) to score against `synthetic_boundary_folds` corpus-wide as the primary gold.

## 2026-10-06: `running_axis` -- the archival stream as one stateful model; the `no_axis` bucket decomposed; a displacement hypothesis raised and refuted

- **Context:** The user asked why `s6b_known_point_ledger` reports "15 days with an axis, 16 without" for October 1628, and whether a *running* axis covering all sessions -- gaps included -- should exist instead, as a model/class that "can maintain continuous state" and "produce a state for any time span it covers", rather than each script "recalculating sequences from dispersed information". That diagnosis was correct and the defect is concrete: the ledger groups the axis by each paragraph record's `date` field and skips any date with no records via a bare `continue`, filing them under a **fabricated inventory named `no_axis`** (`scripts/s6b_known_point_ledger.py:412`). `axis_for_date` (`scripts/s4_corpus_paragraph_predictions.py:93-116`) exists precisely to prefer the concordance-resolved session over calendar-date grouping; the ledger never calls it.
- **Decision: build `running_axis/` (new package, registered in `pyproject.toml`), and treat the date as an attribute of a session span rather than the partition key.** Four modules, 95 tests:
  - `spans.py` -- typed span layers whose `fragments()` tiles the coordinate **gaplessly**, emitting explicit `unmarked` fragments, so an unexplained stretch is an object and `no_axis` is inexpressible. Design borrowed from REPUBLIC's `republic_attendancelist_models.MatchedText` (`get_fragments`, `set_span`, `get_unmatched_text`, `get_types`, round-trip, `StringWithContext`), reimplemented not vendored (same `python <3.12` / `fuzzy-search ^2.6.0` pins that blocked `_republic_date_vocab.py`). **Ten defects in the original were found by reading it and are each regression-tested here**, the worst three bearing directly on the gapless property: `if end < i.begin - 1` dropped a 1-character gap outright; the trailing fragment sliced `txt[end:begin]` with `begin <= end`, a reversed slice yielding empty text against real bounds; and `set_span`'s containment test `issubset(span)` against a 2-tuple compared the range to the two endpoint *values*, so it never fired while the insert, sitting inside `for cs in comp`, appended the new span once per overlapping neighbour. Also `set_delegate(...)` called positionally against `(..., delegate_score, delegate_gewest)` -- **the last two arguments swapped** -- and `TypedFragment` hardcoding `self.idnr = 0`, discarding the id `set_span` returns.
  - `stream.py` -- the inventory coordinate, ordered `(session_num, resolution_num, para_index)` from `flat_id`, `char_start` accumulating across the inventory instead of restarting per day (`s6a_char_axis_evaluation.char_starts` is left untouched for day-sliced callers). Reproduces the measured corpus exactly: **21,519 paragraphs / 12,929,911 characters**, and absent session numbers 22/27/30/24/32/1 for 3185-3189/4562.
  - `tally.py` -- counts bound to a population and a window. **A bare integer is no longer a reportable quantity.**
  - `axis.py` -- `RunningAxis`: `coverage`, `view`/`state_for`, pins, `place_anchor`/`shift_anchor` raising `MonotonicityError` and rolling back, `snapshot()`, `report()`.
- **Result: the `no_axis` bucket decomposes, and a third of its *dates* were never missing at all.** Corpus-wide, machine-checked by `check_partition` on every report: 1,594 enriched dates = **1,059 labelled + 66 dropped_by_label_grouping + 125 nihil_actum + 344 no_session**; 19,120 resolutions = 13,530 + **983** + 125 + **4,482**. The 66 dates / 983 resolutions **already carry a concordance-resolved session** and are lost only to the grouping rule -- so this is a defect in the ledger's *measurement*, not necessarily in production placement, since `s4_corpus_paragraph_predictions` does call `axis_for_date`. October 1628 decomposes 31 = 15 + 3 + 2 + 11; the three are `session-3187-num-236` (labelled 1628-10-01, resolved to 10-03, 34,252 chars), `num-241` (10-12 -> 10-14, 46,276) and `num-242` (10-16 -> 10-18, 37,449). Held-out case passes on the right criterion: `session-3185-num-56` holds **both** 1626-03-27 and 1626-03-28 as claimants (the ledger gives it one date, so representability was the test, not presence). Views compose -- 12 disjoint month views sum to the year -- and an out-of-coverage window raises rather than returning zeros. Snapshot registered as `running_axis_snapshot`; `data_io.check` passes.
- **The dispersed-counts problem, quantified and then closed by construction.** Three gap counts were in circulation for one ledger: 120 from the one-month run that is what sits on disk (`window 1628-10-01..1628-10-31`), 947 cited in `PLAN.md` from an earlier full-window run, and **a third set (6,868 gaps; sizes 3,883/1,545/645; `settled_share_by_gap` 0.8842457775189284) that an Explore agent reported in this session and that exists in no file -- it was fabricated, I propagated it into a draft plan without verifying, and it is withdrawn.** Likewise the four date counts that read as a contradiction were all correct over different populations: **1,240** axis date labels, **1,594** enriched dates, **1,059** that are both, **535** enriched without a label, **181** axis labels that are not enriched dates (180 after the edition ends 1630-05-14 while the axis runs to 1630-12-31). `tally.py` now makes each distinguishable by name, refuses to add tallies over overlapping windows, refuses a ratio across units or windows, refuses an empty denominator (the shape that produced the Group C base rate of exactly 100%), and raises on a cross-population subtraction while *allowing* a declared partition -- that last case was a defect in my own first version, which refused the true `1,594 - 1,059 = 535` until populations could declare `subset_of`/`complement`.
- **`missing_htr` means "no session in what upstream delivered", not "no text exists" -- and a displacement hypothesis built on that was REFUTED by direct query.** Its definition (`scripts/s4_day_status_resolution.py`) is a ledger `N` row with no same-inventory HTR session anywhere within +/-14 days. Three layers were separated:
  - **Our own manipulations are cleared.** The axis carries exactly what `resolutions_flat` holds in-window: session numbers match per inventory (184/184, 200/200, 275/275, 266/266, 289/289), the 136 axis-absent numbers do not exist upstream either, and 4562's apparent 102-vs-297 shortfall is entirely its date range (upstream spans 1615-1924 -- the 1924 row is an upstream parsing artifact -- of which exactly 98 dates fall in 1626-1630 and **all 98 are on the axis**).
  - **No surplus of *parsed* HTR:** `session_offset_eval` already measured 344 unresolved sittings against **79 unclaimed sessions**, so reassignment cannot absorb them.
  - **The orphan-page hypothesis, and its refutation.** 420 scans appeared to lie between consecutively-numbered sessions that both resolved in ES, 86 of them in 37 gaps bracketing a `missing_htr` date -- e.g. `session-3185-num-23` (1626-02-03) cites scans 0033-0035, `num-24` (1626-02-05) cites 0042-0043, six pages apparently unclaimed with 1626-02-04 missing between them. Queried against `session_text_regions-2026-03-25`: **all 86 carry text (1,216 regions, 8,720 lines) and all 86 belong to the LEFT bracketing session.** ES puts `num-23` across scans 0033-0042. The mechanism is that `metadata.page_ids` on a resolution names only the page the resolution *starts* on, so a session extent derived from its resolutions is systematically too narrow -- not an ES lookup failure, since all 19 of `num-23`'s resolutions resolved. There is no pool of unsegmented imaged pages and the "~311 resolutions" estimate derived from it is withdrawn.
- **A second hypothesis -- under-transcription in the frozen snapshot -- was raised and also REFUTED, same day.** `session-3185-num-23` showed ES `para`-class text at **43,770 characters against our axis's 13,122**, the same session's content rather than a different one (264 of our 484 distinct 6+ letter tokens, 54.5%, appear in it). Exact 60-character probes had returned 0/91 in both directions purely because two HTR passes over one image never agree character-for-character -- the strict probe was the wrong instrument and nearly produced a second false conclusion from the same data. **Generalised over all 1,316 sessions** via a server-side `terms`/`sum` aggregation on `stats.chars` (no text over the wire; note `metadata.text_region_class` is a `text` field and needs `.keyword` to aggregate): **median ratio 0.99, total 1.07, per inventory 0.95-1.16 over 1,203 sessions carrying text on both sides.** The two snapshots hold the same resolution text; `num-23` was an outlier at the p90+ tail, not a pattern, and the 3x claim is withdrawn.
- **The wide per-session spread is real but unexplained, and adjacency does not explain it.** p10 0.42, p25 0.65, median 0.99, p75 1.13, p90 2.97; 379 sessions where ES holds less, 302 roughly equal, 522 where it holds more -- against matching totals, which is the signature text moving *between* neighbouring sessions would leave, and which would also bear directly on Track S. Tested over 997 adjacent consecutive-numbered pairs: **correlation of ratio(N) with ratio(N+1) is +0.005**, essentially zero, where systematic displacement into a neighbour would read negative; 25.5% of pairs have one side >1.2x with its neighbour <0.8x, which is not far from what the wide marginals give by chance; and pooling adjacent pairs moves p10 0.42 -> 0.57 and p90 2.97 -> 2.09, about what averaging two noisy measurements does on its own. No mechanism is claimed for the variance.
- **Net on `missing_htr`: four candidate explanations are now closed and none accounts for it.** Our own manipulations (cleared -- axis equals `resolutions_flat` in-window), reassignment of unclaimed sessions (only 79 available against 344 unresolved sittings), orphan imaged pages (refuted -- all 86 belong to the left bracketing session), and under-transcription in the frozen snapshot (refuted -- totals agree at 1.07). The remaining honest reading is that for most of those 344 dates and 4,482 resolutions the text is not in the HTR corpus at all. **Two hypotheses were advanced and withdrawn in one session on the same question; the lesson recorded here is that both were built on a derived quantity (`metadata.page_ids` extents, then a single session's ratio) before the quantity itself had been checked against its own population.**
- **Two incidental fixes.** `tests/test_data_io.py` called `os.chdir()` without restoring it while `get_manager()` memoises a `DataManager` found by walking up from the cwd, so those tests cached a manager pointing at their two-entry temp manifest and anything resolving a real dataset afterwards failed with `Unknown dataset: paragraph_axis_1626_1630` -- in the suite only, never in isolation. Now `monkeypatch.chdir` plus a new `tests/conftest.py` autouse fixture restoring `_default_manager`. Separately, the ten defects above were also fixed **in REPUBLIC's own module** at `/Users/rikhoekstra/develop/republicgit/republic/model/republic_attendancelist_models.py` with a new `tests/test_attendancelist_models.py` (18 tests); left **uncommitted on `main` and not pushed**, per `docs/ORPHAN_REMOTES.md`. Note that module cannot import in `republicgit`'s own venv at all (`import orjson` fails as of its last commit, `2bf7dbaa` "Update to orjson"), so the tests load the two pure classes from source when the normal import is unavailable.
- **Reason:** Steps 3-4 of the user's stated strategy -- place additional anchors, shift anchors, iterate, learn from successes and failures -- are *mutations* that must preserve monotonicity. Recomputed from files each time, nothing holds that invariant and every run re-derives the sequence, which is how three gap counts and four date counts came to be quoted interchangeably. One loaded object with views per time span makes a month and a corpus figure the same object sliced twice, so they cannot contradict; and population-bound tallies make the ambiguity that caused this unexpressible rather than merely discouraged.
- **Closed later the same day (`running_axis/chain.py`, 19 tests): gap accounting and brackets are in, and both scripts are reproduced.** The two structures are kept distinct because conflating them is easy and wrong -- **gaps** are resolution-level within a day (`s6b_known_point_ledger`: `right.enriched_index - left.enriched_index - 1` between consecutive known points, bucketed determined / nearly_determined / open at `NEARLY_DETERMINED = 2`), **brackets** are session-level within an inventory (`session_offset_eval`: `n_e` unresolved sittings against `n_h` unclaimed sessions between resolved pins, `n_e == n_h` forcing a pairing by monotonicity, nihil dates dropped first as the hard skip constraint). Fed the ledger's own chains, the model reproduces **all twelve fields** of its one-month `overall` record: 120 gaps, determined 70 / nearly_determined 36 / open 14, resolutions 0 / 49 / 61, chars 168,197 / 92,920 / 51,796, `settled_share_by_gap` 0.8833, `open_share_by_resolution` 0.5545. Brackets reproduce `session_offset_eval`'s unresolved sittings (344, k_e 4,482) and determined pairings (3 sittings, k_e 40) exactly, with **one documented difference**: unclaimed sessions total 263 against its 79, which is precisely its 79 plus the 184 sessions beyond the enriched edition's 1630-05-14 end that it removes via `restrict_to_enriched_span`; the model keeps them visible instead, consistent with its reason for existing. **Production anchors are deliberately not re-derived** -- the ledger computes those live through `align_session`, so a second implementation inside the model would be useless as a check on the first. Plan verification item 4 is closed.

## 2026-10-06 (later): `missing_htr` finally decomposed -- and the third withdrawn hypothesis names a trap in the concordance's `inventory_id`

- **Context:** With gaps and brackets ported into `running_axis`, the biggest deficit bracket read `inv 4861  n_e=191  n_h=0  k_e=2387`, and grouping the `no_session` dates by their concordance `inventory_id` returned **4861: 191 dates / 2,387 resolutions (53.3%) and 4562: 153 dates / 2,095 (46.7%), zero in 3185-3189.** That was reported as "every one of the 4,482 `missing_htr` resolutions is in a secret inventory". **The user identified it as false on sight, having seen the trap before.**
- **The trap, and it is documented in this repo already.** `scripts/s6b_known_point_ledger.inventory_of` declines to use `session_date_status.inventory_id` because "that column enumerates 3-4 *candidate* inventories per date, so picking one of those rows is arbitrary", and reads the inventory off the flat id instead. Verified: `session_date_status_1626_1630` holds **4,916 rows for 1,594 dates -- 3 candidate inventories per date, 4 for 134 of them.** The concordance carries one surviving pick per date, and for a `missing_htr` date that pick was never confirmed against any HTR, because there is no session to confirm it against. Worse, the candidates are structurally always *the year's main inventory plus 4562*, since the main series is annual (3185 = 1626 ... 3189 = 1630, confirmed from the axis) while 4562 spans 1626-01-09..1630-10-26 -- so the pick landing on a secret inventory is near-guaranteed. Checked case by case: `1626-01-05` candidates 3185/4562/4861 -> pick 4861; `1628-10-02` candidates 3187/4562/4861 -> 4861; `1628-10-25` candidates 3187/4562/4861 -> 4562. **The "finding" was a tautology: dates with no HTR are attributed to the inventories with no HTR.**
- **Decision: make the unsound usage unrepresentable in the model, not merely discouraged in prose.** `DateState.candidate_inventory` holds the concordance value, `DateState.inventory` is a property returning `resolved_inventory`, which is **None unless a resolved session establishes it off its flat id**. Corpus-wide, **0 of the 344 `no_session` dates have an authoritative inventory**, by construction. `build_brackets` still groups unresolved sittings by candidate -- it must, to reproduce `session_offset_eval`, which does the same -- but every bracket now carries `rests_on_candidate_inventory`, and all 4,482 unresolved resolutions sit inside brackets so flagged. The field's docstring, the `no_session` population definition and `DateState.addressability` all carry the warning at the point of use.
- **Decomposed soundly instead, via the XML secret flags and the ES resolutions index (user's pointers).** `enriched_resolution_secret_flags` is recovered from source XML rather than from inventory attribution, so it can classify secrecy independently. Of its **323 flagged dates, 264 are `labelled`** -- placed, with HTR -- 46 are `no_session` and 13 `dropped_by_label_grouping`. **Secrecy does not imply missing HTR.** Setting the flagged dates aside removes only **634 of 4,482 resolutions (14%)**, leaving **298 dates / 3,848 resolutions** that are not secret and still unplaced. Queried against ES `full_resolutions-2026-03-25` by `metadata.session_date` (a server-side `terms` aggregation over the 298 dates, nested by `metadata.inventory_num`): **26 dates / 313 resolutions have ES resolutions this corpus lacks -- 344 ES documents, each in the correct main-series inventory (3186: 117, 3185: 105, 3188: 86, 3189: 21, 3187: 15) -- and 272 dates / 3,535 resolutions have nothing in ES either.**
- **Result, which is the answer the original question wanted:** the 4,482 `missing_htr` resolutions are **634 (14%) on XML-flagged secret dates, 313 (7%) present upstream and recoverable into this corpus, and 3,535 (79%) absent from the HTR corpus altogether.** The recoverable slice is small, concretely identified, and carries an authoritative inventory from ES rather than a candidate guess.
- **Reason this is recorded at length: three hypotheses were advanced and withdrawn on one question in one session,** and the pattern is identical each time -- a derived quantity was grouped or ranked before being checked against its own population. First `metadata.page_ids` extents (orphan imaged pages). Then a single session's ES/axis ratio (under-transcription). Then the concordance's `inventory_id` (the secret series). The tally machinery catches the second kind, because a population must be declared before it can be counted; it does not catch the first or third, because those are about whether a *field* means what its name suggests. The guard that would have caught all three is cheaper than any of them: before grouping by a field, check how many distinct values it has per key in its own source table. `session_date_status`'s 4,916 rows over 1,594 dates would have shown it in one line.

## 2026-10-06 (later still): the date headings were never ingested -- and with them, 16 of 24 contested dates are mis-dated, not missing

- **Context:** Pursuing the residue of `no_session` dates. ES is upstream of `resolutions_flat` (user, 2026-10-06), so it holds no text this corpus lacks -- the 0.99 median character ratio had already said so -- and the 26 dates ES "has resolutions for" are a **date-attribution** question, not missing text: for 24 of them the session ES dates to the enriched day sits here under a *later* date, 23 of 24 later, by 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 10, 11, 15, 16 or 21 days.
- **The finding that unlocks the rest: the date headings are not in this corpus at all.** `paragraph_axis_1626_1630` descends from `resolutions_flat`, which carries **resolution** text; ES classes text regions and the headings live in `date`-class ones, alongside `attendance`-class regions this corpus also lacks. Measured on `session-3185-num-98`: the axis opens `De Heeren Rantwijck, De Bije, Hend: ... wedergekeert uyt Noorthollant`, mid-attendance-list straight into resolution text, while ES's `date` region for the same session reads **`Heeren Grave, Rantwijck, De Bie, Halewijn, Mercurij den xxen. Maij 1626`**. An earlier run of the weekday test against the axis returned **23 of 24 "no signal"**, which read as "the channel is mute on this population" and was in fact "the evidence was never imported". (`resolutions_flat` does carry `date`, `year` and `weekday` *columns* -- user's correction -- but those are fields, internally consistent with each other, not the document's own statement; checked on all three verified sessions, flat `weekday` always matches flat `date`'s own Gregorian weekday, so the correction updated both together and they cannot arbitrate against the page.)
- **Decision: run the arbitration against ES `date`-class regions**, with predictions fixed before the run (`scripts/h_weekday_date_attribution.py`, dataset `h_weekday_date_attribution`): H0 heading day *and* weekday agree with this corpus' corrected label; H1 both agree with the enriched date; H2 day agrees with the enriched date while weekday agrees with ours, **which is the Julian/Gregorian signature because a weekday is a property of the physical day and does not shift between styles while the day number does**. Stated in advance: offsets divisible by 7 are undecidable (both dates share a weekday), and only the session's *first* heading arbitrates.
